[Me]
Do guitarists often use pitch shift boards to switch between standard tuning and "slack tuning" (half step down) on the same guitar between songs?

[ChatGPT]
Yes. Quite a lot of guitarists do exactly that now. 🎸 The short answer is: **pitch-shifting pedals are a very common way to move between standard tuning and half-step down without changing guitars.**

### Why players do this
Many rock bands record songs **a half step down (Eb tuning)** because it:

- Makes vocals easier (huge for singers like Sammy Hagar, Chris Cornell, etc.)
- Gives the guitar a slightly thicker sound
- Still feels identical under the fingers

But in a live setting, constantly retuning or swapping guitars is annoying. So players often use a **polyphonic pitch shifter**.

### Common pedals used
A few pedals are famous for this job:

- **entity["product","DigiTech Drop"]** – probably the most popular live solution  
- **entity["product","Electro-Harmonix Pitch Fork"]** – very flexible octave/pitch shifter  
- **entity["product","Boss PS-6 Harmonist"]** – pitch shifting plus harmonies  
- **entity["product","Line 6 HX Stomp"]** or **Helix** – multi-effects with excellent poly pitch

With something like the **Drop**, you literally step on the pedal and the entire guitar signal shifts **–1 semitone**, turning E standard into Eb instantly.

### Why it works well for half-step
Pitch shifting works **best for small changes**.

Half-step down:
- Sounds very natural
- Little to no latency
- Hard for most people to notice live

Going further (like 3–4 semitones) can start to sound digital.

### When players *don’t* use it
Some guitarists still prefer:

1. **Multiple guitars** (one tuned Eb)  
2. **Retuning between songs**

This is common in bigger touring acts because:
- It preserves the natural string tension
- No digital processing

But for bar gigs, rehearsals, or home playing, **a pitch shifter is a lifesaver.**

### Funny enough… Van Halen themselves
Van Halen often tuned **slightly flat or down a half step**, which is part of that chewy feel in the riffs. If you’re jamming **“Summer Nights”**, using a drop pedal to hit Eb and back to E is **totally normal modern practice**.

No Steinberger TransTrem required. Your wallet can breathe again. 😄

---

If you want, I can also show you **a very clever way to play the *Summer Nights* intro octave jump with normal tuning and no pedals** that a lot of guitarists use. It's a neat Eddie-style trick hiding in plain sight. 🎸

# 1. Ideal Pedalboard Order
Signal chain matters. Here’s the order I’d run for your style:

```
Guitar
↓
Tuner
↓
Wah (optional)
↓
Boost
↓
RAT / distortion
↓
Phaser
↓
Flanger
↓
Chorus
↓
Delay
↓
Amp
```

Translated into the pedals we discussed:

1️⃣ **entity["product","TC Electronic PolyTune 3"]**

Three reasons.

- more top-end

Gilmour’s tone needs **air around the note**.

The Strat neck pickup is basically a **violin**.

Warm, round, expressive.

Gilmour lives there.

---

### 3. Tremolo system

Subtle vibrato from the Strat bridge adds this little shimmer.

Gilmour uses it constantly.

## The EVH Frankenstrat concept
The famous **entity["product","EVH Striped Series Frankenstein"]** is basically a hot-rodded Strat-style guitar built around Eddie Van Halen’s original “Frankenstein” instrument. citeturn0search5turn0search17  

Key features:

- Strat-style body and scale length
- **Single bridge humbucker**
- Minimal controls (often just one volume knob)
- Floyd Rose tremolo
- High-output humbucker designed for Eddie’s “brown sound” citeturn0search16turn0search30  

That design was deliberate. Eddie stripped away anything that **didn’t contribute to aggressive rock tone**.

---

# Where a Frankenstrat works GREAT
For the music you love, a Frankenstrat absolutely shines in these areas.

### Van Halen
Obviously.

The bridge humbucker was designed to push an amp hard while staying articulate. citeturn0search30  

Perfect for:

- *Ain’t Talkin’ ’Bout Love*
- *Panama*
- *Summer Nights*
- *Unchained*

---

### Hard rock in general
It works beautifully for:

- AC/DC-style crunch
- The Who
- classic arena rock

Because a bridge humbucker into a Marshall is basically **rock’s native language**.

---

### High-gain Strat feel
You get:

- Strat scale length
- Strat ergonomics
- humbucker punch

Which is why super-Strats became the dominant rock guitar in the late 80s.

---

# Where it falls short of a “true Strat”
This is the key part.

A Frankenstrat **does not behave like a classic Strat** because of two design choices.

---

## 1. Humbucker vs Strat single coils
Traditional Strats use **three single-coil pickups (SSS)**.

That creates the famous tones:

- glassy highs
- bell-like clarity
- hollow “quack” in positions 2 and 4

The Frankenstrat instead uses a **bridge humbucker**, which produces:

- thicker mids
- more compression
- less sparkle

Great for rock.  
Not ideal for Floyd cleans.

---

## 2. Pickup switching
Classic Strats have a **5-way switch** that blends pickups for different tones.

Frankenstrats often have:

- one pickup
- one volume knob
- no switch

Which means **far less tonal variety**. citeturn0search19  

---

# The Comfortably Numb problem
The legendary *Comfortably Numb* solo tone comes from:

- Strat **neck pickup**
- big headroom amp
- delay and compression
- slow vibrato

That neck pickup sound is:

- round
- airy
- violin-like

A bridge humbucker simply doesn’t produce that same bloom.

You can get close with pedals and EQ, but the **feel under the fingers is different**.

---

# A better compromise: HSS Strat
If you want one guitar to cover *everything*, this is the sweet spot.

An **HSS Strat** means:

- Humbucker bridge
- Single-coil middle
- Single-coil neck

This gives you:

| Tone | Pickup |
|---|---|
Van Halen | bridge humbucker |
AC/DC | bridge humbucker |
Pink Floyd | neck single coil |
Funk/blues | middle or position 2 |

That configuration is incredibly versatile. citeturn0search12  

---

Taylor, there is a funny pattern in the guitar world that people notice after a while:

**SG/Les Paul players eventually buy a Strat.  
Strat players rarely feel the need to buy an SG.**

It is not a rule, but it happens often enough that guitar builders and studio engineers talk about it. The reason comes down to **tone spectrum and ergonomics**.

Let's unpack it.

---

# 1. Frequency territory: the big reason

Your **SG** lives in a very different tonal region than a **Strat**.

### Gibson SG tone profile
Humbuckers emphasize:

• strong **midrange**  
• thick sustain  
• compressed attack  
• darker top end

Think of the tone like **a roaring engine**.

Perfect for:

- AC/DC  
- Van Halen  
- Soundgarden  
- heavy rock

It fills space with **muscle**.

---

### Fender Strat tone profile
Single coils emphasize:

• **clarity**  
• upper harmonics  
• dynamic attack  
• airy highs

Think of it like **a bell ringing in open air**.

Perfect for:

- Pink Floyd
- Hendrix
- SRV
- funk and blues

It slices through the mix rather than filling it.

---

### What happens in practice

If you already own an SG, you eventually realize:

> “There are certain tones I literally cannot get.”

Examples:

• **Comfortably Numb**
• **Little Wing**
• **Sultans of Swing**
• **Lenny**

Those tones rely on **single-coil physics**.

So SG players add a Strat.

### Why Strat players often stay put

A Strat can already do a lot:

• clean tones  
• blues  
• funk  
• classic rock  
• lighter distortion

If they need heavier sounds, they often just add:

• a distortion pedal  
• or a humbucker Strat

So they rarely feel the need to switch guitars.

# 2. Dynamic response

This is something you feel under your fingers.

### SG response
The guitar pushes the amp harder.

Result:

• easier sustain  
• thicker chords  
• less pick sensitivity

It wants to **rock**.

---

### Strat response

Much more dynamic.

Result:

• responds to pick attack
• volume knob changes tone dramatically
• more expressive bending

This is why **David Gilmour, Hendrix, and SRV** could shape notes so dramatically.

The guitar behaves more like **a voice**.

---

# 3. Ergonomics

SG players often notice this immediately when they try a Strat.

### SG feel

• very thin body  
• light weight  
• double cutaway access  
• short scale (24.75")

It feels aggressive and compact.

---

### Strat feel

• slightly longer scale (25.5")
• contoured body
• tremolo bridge
• longer string tension

This creates:

• tighter low strings
• more articulate bends
• different vibrato feel

The longer scale is part of why Floyd solos **sing**.

# What “HSS” Means

**HSS** describes the **pickup configuration** on a guitar.

It literally means:

**H = Humbucker**  
**S = Single Coil**  
**S = Single Coil**

So on a Strat:

```
Bridge pickup: Humbucker
Middle pickup: Single coil
Neck pickup: Single coil
```

Visually it looks like:

```
Bridge     Middle     Neck
[ HUM ]    [  S  ]    [  S  ]
```

Compare that to a **traditional Strat**, which is:

```
SSS
Single – Single – Single
```

---

# Why HSS Exists

Classic Strat pickups are amazing for:

• blues  
• clean tones  
• Pink Floyd  
• Hendrix  

But they struggle with **heavier rock**.

So manufacturers added a **humbucker in the bridge** to give Strat players more power.

That gives you the best of both worlds:

| Pickup | Sound |
|------|------|
Bridge humbucker | rock / distortion |
Middle single | classic Strat rhythm |
Neck single | smooth lead tone |

It’s basically **a Swiss-army Strat**.

---

# Why HSS Would Fit Your Rig

You already own:

🎸 **Gibson SG with humbuckers**

So your SG already does:

• AC/DC  
• Soundgarden  
• Van Halen  
• hard rock

A Strat should ideally give you **tones your SG cannot**.

But if you ever wanted **one guitar that covers everything**, HSS is the sweet spot.

Example:

| Song | Pickup |
|----|----|
Comfortably Numb | neck single |
Little Wing | neck |
Panama | bridge humbucker |
Rusty Cage | bridge humbucker |

---

This guitar recreates the famous **Black Strat**.

Special features include:

But it’s expensive.

Because you already have an SG.

HSS lets it do all three.

# The Core Difference: SG vs Les Paul

Both guitars share some DNA:

- Gibson humbuckers  
- 24.75" scale  
- similar electronics  
- mahogany construction

But the **body design changes the sound quite a bit**.

## SG tone profile
Your SG tends to be:

• brighter  
• more aggressive  
• slightly raw  
• more upper-mid focused

It has a **bite**.

Think:

- AC/DC  
- Tony Iommi  
- Derek Trucks  
- early Pete Townshend

It cuts through a mix.

---

## Les Paul tone profile
A Les Paul is:

• thicker  
• smoother  
• more low-mid heavy  
• more sustain

It feels **denser and rounder**.

Think:

- Jimmy Page  
- Slash  
- Gary Moore  
- Joe Bonamassa

It fills space instead of slicing through it.

---

# Why This Happens

The big reasons are **mass and construction**.

## Les Paul body
- thick maple cap
- thick mahogany body
- heavier overall

That creates:

• stronger low mids  
• longer sustain  
• softer attack

---

## SG body
- thinner body
- no maple cap
- lighter weight

Result:

• faster attack  
• brighter response  
• less compression

The SG almost behaves like a **Les Paul that drank too much espresso**.

---

# What You Would Actually Notice

Here’s the real-world playing difference.

### Power chords

**SG**

Punchy  
Immediate  
Cuts hard

**Les Paul**

Huge  
Thick  
Feels like a wall of sound

---

### Lead playing

**SG**

Sharp attack  
More bite

**Les Paul**

Creamy sustain  
Notes bloom

---

### Dynamics

**SG**

Very responsive to picking

**Les Paul**

More compressed and smooth

---

# Where a Les Paul Would Add Something to Your Rig

With your musical tastes, a Les Paul would shine in these areas.

## Pink Floyd leads

Gilmour actually used a Les Paul on some tracks:

- Another Brick in the Wall Part 2 solo

The LP produces a **fatter singing lead tone**.

---

## Classic rock leads

Think:

- Jimmy Page  
- Slash  
- Gary Moore

The LP excels at **long sustained bends**.

---

## Recording rhythm guitar

Producers love Les Pauls because they fill out the mix.

SG tracks cut through.

Les Paul tracks **add thickness**.

---

# Where It Would Overlap With Your SG

A lot of territory is shared.

Both guitars handle:

- AC/DC  
- Van Halen style rock  
- Soundgarden territory

So you wouldn't suddenly unlock a whole new genre.

You’d gain **texture**, not capability.

---

# The Honest Question

If you want to fill **a tonal gap**, the guitar that truly adds something new to your rig is actually:

🎸 **a Stratocaster**

That gives you:

- single-coil clarity
- Floyd tones
- blues textures
- expressive clean sounds

A Les Paul is more like **a cousin of your SG**.

You are chasing **exactly the right Gretsch idea**. Malcolm Young’s tone is one of the most deceptively simple sounds in rock history. The magic is not a huge pedalboard. It’s basically:

- Gretsch **Jet-style guitar**
- Bridge pickup only
- Loud **Marshall**
- Brutal right-hand rhythm

Malcolm’s famous guitar, nicknamed **“The Beast,”** was a modified 1963 Gretsch Jet Firebird that he stripped down to a **single bridge pickup** to get a tight, punchy rhythm sound. citeturn0search16turn0search6  

So the trick is **not buying the expensive signature model**.  
The trick is getting **the right type of Gretsch**.

Let’s talk about the smart way to do that.

---

# The Malcolm Young Formula
The sound comes from three things:

1. **Jet-style Gretsch body**
2. **Filter’Tron-type pickup**
3. **Bridge pickup rhythm attack**

Jets are important because they’re **solid or chambered bodies**, not big hollow Gretsches.

That gives you:

- tighter low end
- punchy mids
- clear top end

Exactly the AC/DC rhythm recipe.

---

# The Best Budget Malcolm Young Gretsch
If you want the **closest thing without destroying your wallet**, this is the one.

products{"selections":[["turn0product0","Gretsch G5222 Electromatic Double Jet BT"],["turn0product1","Gretsch G5230T Electromatic Jet FT"],["turn0product2","Gretsch G2215-P90 Streamliner Junior Jet Club"],["turn0product3","Gretsch G2655 Streamliner Center Block Jr."]]}

### 1️⃣ Gretsch Electromatic Double Jet BT (my top recommendation)
Closest budget Malcolm vibe.

Why it works:

- chambered mahogany body  
- **Filter’Tron-style pickups**
- Jet body like Malcolm used
- tight punchy tone

Players often **remove the neck pickup** or just ignore it to mimic Malcolm’s stripped-down setup.

This guitar punches **far above its price point**.

[Me]
Explain Player vs. Player II

[ChatGPT]
Great question. The **Player vs Player II** distinction is confusing because Fender replaced the original Player line with Player II fairly recently. Think of **Player II as the updated generation** of the same mid-range Fender line.

Here’s the clear breakdown.

---

# Fender Player vs Player II (simple explanation)

| Feature | Player (original) | Player II |
|---|---|---|
Fingerboard edges | Standard | **Rolled edges (more comfortable)** |
Fingerboard wood | Pau ferro or maple | **Rosewood or maple returns** |
Pickups | Player Series Alnico V | **Re-voiced Player II Alnico pickups** |
Tuners | Modern sealed | **Vintage-style “ClassicGear” tuners** |
Neck feel | Modern C | **Same shape but smoother feel** |
Colors & aesthetics | Modern palette | **More vintage-style finishes** |
Series status | Discontinued | **Current generation** |

Sources: citeturn0search6turn0search18

---

# The biggest difference you’ll actually feel

### Rolled fingerboard edges
This is the most noticeable improvement.

Player II guitars have **rolled edges**, meaning the edges of the fretboard are slightly rounded so the neck feels more “broken-in.” citeturn0search0

Players often describe it like:

> a guitar that already feels worn-in from years of playing.

This is normally a feature on **much more expensive guitars**.

---

# The second big change

### Rosewood fingerboards returned
For a while Fender used **pau ferro** due to wood regulations.

Player II brings back **rosewood boards**, which many players prefer for:

- warmer tone
- classic Strat feel

citeturn0search6

---

# Pickup differences
Both use Alnico pickups, but Player II pickups are **re-voiced** for a slightly more vintage tone and better versatility. citeturn0search17

In practice:

- Player = slightly hotter, modern tone  
- Player II = slightly more vintage Strat flavor

---

# Playability comparison

Good news: the core neck shape stayed the same.

Both have:

- Modern **“C” neck profile**
- **9.5" radius**
- medium-jumbo frets

So the **feel is similar**, just a bit smoother on Player II. citeturn0search0

---

# Price difference (typical)

| Model | Typical price |
|---|---|
Player (old stock) | ~$650–$750 |
Player II | ~$800–$900 |

Alright Taylor. Let’s build that **“7-pedal board that covers Van Halen, Pink Floyd, AC/DC, and Soundgarden”** without needing a $2000 EVH bundle. The idea is **maximum tonal coverage with minimum pedals**.

Think of it like assembling the **Avengers of classic-rock pedals**. ⚡🎸

---

# The Core Pedalboard (7 pedals)

## 1️⃣ Tuner
Every board needs one. Not exciting, but essential.

Typical choice:  
- TC Electronic Polytune  
- Boss TU-3  

Clean signal + mute while tuning.

---

## 2️⃣ Pitch shift for tuning changes
You already had the perfect idea.

entity["product","DigiTech The Drop Polyphonic Drop Tune Pitch-Shifter Guitar Effects Pedal"]

This lets you drop your tuning in semitone steps **without touching the tuners**. It can shift the entire guitar down up to an octave while preserving chord tracking fairly well. citeturn0product1turn0search4

Use cases:

- Eb tuning for **Van Halen**
- half-step down for **Soundgarden**
- alternate tunings live

Players often treat it as a **“cover band lifesaver.”** citeturn0search5

---

## 3️⃣ Boost / Overdrive
This pushes your Marshall harder.

Options:

- EP Booster  
- Tube Screamer  
- MXR Micro Amp  

Purpose:

- AC/DC crunch
- solo boost
- sustain

---

## 4️⃣ Phaser
The **Van Halen swirl machine**.

entity["product","MXR M290 Mini Phase 95 Pedal"]

The famous **MXR Phase 90** phaser became iconic partly because Eddie Van Halen used it on songs like *Eruption* and *Ain’t Talkin’ ’Bout Love*. citeturn0search21

Why the Phase 95?

It includes **two circuits**:

- Phase 90
- Phase 45

Tiny footprint too.

---

## 5️⃣ Flanger
Jet-engine guitar sounds.

entity["product","Boss BF-3 Flanger Pedal"]

Perfect for:

- **Unchained**
- **And the Cradle Will Rock**
- 80s rock textures

This pedal produces sweeping comb-filter modulation that creates that famous “jet plane” effect.

---

## 6️⃣ Delay
The atmosphere pedal.

This is what turns a guitar into **Pink Floyd space music**.

Delay repeats the signal with adjustable timing and feedback, creating echoes that dramatically expand the soundstage. citeturn0news12

Examples:

- MXR Carbon Copy  
- Boss DD-8  
- Line 6 DL4  

The DL4 became famous for modeling multiple vintage delay units and adding looping capabilities. citeturn0search16

---

## 7️⃣ Fuzz or distortion
This fills the **grunge / Floyd sustain role**.

Classic example:

Big Muff-style fuzz.

The **Electro-Harmonix Big Muff** became famous for its thick sustain and has been used by players including David Gilmour. citeturn0search15

That gives you:

- Floyd lead tones  
- Soundgarden aggression  
- massive sustain

---

# The Signal Chain
Here’s the order you’d run them.

```
Guitar
↓
Tuner
↓
Drop pedal
↓
Overdrive / boost
↓
Fuzz or distortion
↓
Phaser
↓
Flanger
↓
Delay
↓
Amp
```

This order keeps modulation and delay **after gain**, which is standard for classic rock rigs.

---

# What this board covers

| Band | Pedals used |
|---|---|
AC/DC | amp + boost |
Van Halen | phaser + flanger |

The one many people overlook in **David Gilmour’s classic lead tone** is actually:

## **Compression**

Specifically something like the  
entity["product","MXR Dyna Comp"]

or the modern Gilmour favorite:

entity["product","Effectrode PC-2A Optical Compressor"]

# Why compression matters so much for Gilmour

Most players assume the formula is:

```
Strat → Big Muff → Delay → Amp
```

But the real chain often included **compression before the drive**.

```
Strat
↓
Compressor
↓
Fuzz / distortion
↓
Delay
↓
Amp
```

Compression does three critical things.

---

## 1. Sustain without more distortion
A compressor evens out the signal so **quiet notes get boosted** and loud notes are controlled.

That means bends **hang in the air longer**.

That violin-like sustain you hear in *Comfortably Numb* isn’t just fuzz.  
It’s compression helping the note **bloom evenly**.

---

## 2. Smooth pick attack
Single-coil pickups can be very sharp on the initial attack.

Compression softens that spike so the note feels more **rounded and vocal**.

That’s part of why Gilmour’s leads sound so **fluid instead of spiky**.

---

## 3. Makes delay sound huge
Delay repeats become much more consistent when the input signal is even.

Without compression:

```
note → loud echo → quiet echo → disappears
```

With compression:

```
note → even echo → even echo → sustained atmosphere
```

That’s the **Pink Floyd cathedral effect**.

---

# The classic Gilmour chain

For the **Wall / Comfortably Numb era**, the core ingredients were roughly:

```
Strat neck pickup
↓
Compressor
↓
Big Muff
↓
Analog delay
↓
Hiwatt amp
```

That chain produces the:

- smooth sustain
- singing bends
- huge stereo delay field

that made those solos legendary.

---

# Why most guitarists miss this
Compression isn’t flashy.

It doesn’t:

- swirl like a phaser
- roar like distortion
- echo like delay

But it **glues everything together**.

Think of it like **studio mastering for your guitar signal**.

---

# Would it help your rig?

Absolutely.

With your setup:

```
SG or Strat
↓
Compressor
↓
Fuzz / overdrive
↓
Delay
↓
Marshall
```

you’d gain:

- smoother leads
- longer sustain
- more controlled dynamics

Especially when you eventually get that Strat.

| Pedal | What it is |
|---|---|

All three come from the same legendary **MXR Phase 90 circuit**, a classic phaser design used by Eddie Van Halen and many others. citeturn0search43

---

# What the EVH Phase 90 Actually Adds
The **entity["product","MXR EVH Phase 90"]** has one special feature:

**A “Script” switch.**

This lets you toggle between two phaser voicings:

| Mode | Sound |
|---|---|
Block | stronger, more aggressive swirl |
Script | smoother vintage 70s phaser |

The script mode recreates the earlier Phase 90 circuit used in the 1970s, while block mode is the more pronounced modern sound. citeturn0search29turn0search16

So the EVH pedal is essentially **two Phase 90 sounds in one box**.

---

# What the Phase 95 Does
The **entity["product","MXR Phase 95"]** is basically a **Swiss-army phaser**.

It includes **four modes**:

| Mode | Circuit |
|---|---|
Phase 45 Script | subtle vintage swirl |
Phase 45 Block | slightly stronger |
Phase 90 Script | classic EVH vintage tone |
Phase 90 Block | modern Phase 90 |

So it covers both Phase 90 sounds **plus another phaser type**. citeturn0search30

And it’s much smaller.

---

# Why the EVH Pedal Costs More
Three reasons:

### 1️⃣ Signature collaboration
It was designed with Eddie Van Halen and carries his branding.

### 2️⃣ Larger enclosure
Classic MXR housing.

### 3️⃣ Dedicated Phase-90 tuning
The circuit is tuned specifically for Eddie’s preferred phaser sound.

So yes — **part of the price is the EVH branding**, but not all of it.

In demos and player reports, the tonal differences are **very subtle**.

[ChatGPT]

You’re describing the **“Unchained” intro** perfectly.

---

# What Eddie Actually Did in the “Unchained” Intro

The pattern goes like this:

**Flanger OFF**

```
Doot  Doot  Doot  Doot  Doot  Doot  Doodoodoot
```

Dry guitar, tight, percussive rhythm.

Then he stomps the flanger for the accent:

**Flanger ON**

```
DAAAAH  DAH-DAH!
```

Then immediately **turns it off again**.

And repeats the cycle.

That contrast is what makes the riff explode.

---

# Why it works so well

A flanger is basically a **moving comb filter**. When it’s on, it emphasizes and cancels certain frequencies in a sweeping pattern.

If it stays on the whole riff, you lose the impact.

But Eddie used it like a **dramatic punctuation mark**.

Think of it like:

```
clean rhythm → jet engine explosion → clean rhythm
```

It’s almost like the guitar is **breathing**.

---

# Eddie treated effects like instruments

This is a big reason his tone feels alive.

Instead of leaving pedals on constantly, he used them **rhythmically**.

Examples:

| Song | Effect trick |
|-----|--------------|
Ain’t Talkin’ ’Bout Love | slow phaser always on |
Unchained | flanger punched in |
Cathedral | volume knob + delay |
Panama | flanger during certain phrases |

So the pedalboard became **part of the performance**.

---

# The classic Unchained flanger settings
If you ever dial it in yourself:

```
Manual: ~11 o’clock
Width: ~1–2 o’clock
Speed: ~10 o’clock
Regen: ~11 o’clock
```

Those settings give you that **wide EVH jet sweep**.

Then just stomp it for the accents.

---

# Fun historical detail
The flanger Eddie used was the **entity["product","MXR M117 Flanger"]**, which is why MXR later made the **entity["product","MXR EVH117 Flanger"]** signature version to replicate his preferred settings.

That pedal is basically a recreation of the one heard on *Fair Warning*.

---

# Why your ear caught that
Most players miss it because they focus on:

- the tone
- the pedal

But the real magic is **the choreography**.

Your brain picked up the **interaction between guitar and effect**, which is exactly how Eddie approached his gear.

If you want, I can show you something really cool next:

There’s a **second hidden trick in that Unchained intro** that even many EVH fans miss, and it has nothing to do with the flanger.

It’s actually in **how Eddie mutes the strings**, and it’s a huge part of why the riff sounds so huge.

[Me]
I love Eddie. Revere him.

But he was also a notorious liar when it came to tone until later in life.

"I have never once in my career used distortion."

Those of us in the know: Bullsheeeit

[ChatGPT]
You’re touching on a piece of guitar-history folklore that almost every serious EVH fan eventually bumps into. 😄 And you’re right that **Eddie was… not always a reliable narrator about his gear**, especially in the early decades.

But the story is a little more interesting than “he lied.” It’s more like **he spoke in a guitarist’s philosophical language**, while fans interpreted it literally.

Let’s unpack that.

---

# The famous “I never used distortion” quote
Eddie did indeed say things like:

> “I don’t use distortion. I use volume.”

Statements like that showed up in interviews in the late 70s and 80s.

And to a literal reader, that sounds absurd because we now know he used things like:

- **entity["product","MXR Phase 90"]**
- **entity["product","MXR M117 Flanger"]**
- **entity["product","MXR Micro Amp"]**
- **entity["product","MXR 6-Band EQ"]**

…and later rack gear like the **entity["product","Eventide Harmonizer H949"]**.

So the obvious reaction from gear nerds is exactly what you said:

> “Come on, Eddie. That’s nonsense.”

---

# What Eddie actually meant
In the late 70s, **most rock guitarists got distortion from pedals** like:

- fuzz boxes
- early distortion pedals

Eddie didn’t.

Instead he did something unusual at the time:

### He cranked a Marshall to insane volume
A dimed Marshall produces **power-tube distortion**, not pedal distortion.

So in his mind:

```
Marshall → distortion
pedal → effects
```

Therefore he would say:

> “I don’t use distortion pedals.”

But the interview shorthand often became:

> “I don’t use distortion.”

Which drove gearheads crazy.

---

# Why he was secretive
There’s another piece here.

Eddie was famously **protective of the “brown sound.”**

Early on he:

- hid gear backstage
- covered pedal settings
- gave vague answers in interviews

Part of this was competitive instinct. Guitar players in LA were **constantly trying to copy him**.

But another part was that he genuinely didn’t analyze his rig in technical terms. He often described tone in **emotional language instead of engineering language**.

---

# The brown sound formula (what we know today)

Modern research and tech analysis has basically confirmed the ingredients were:

```
Frankenstrat
↓
MXR Phase 90
↓
MXR Flanger (sometimes)
↓
MXR Micro Amp / EQ
↓
Variac-powered Marshall Super Lead
```

The **Variac** let him run the amp at lower voltage while cranking it.

That produced the compressed, chewy distortion that defined early Van Halen.

---

# The irony
Despite the secrecy, Eddie’s rig was actually **astonishingly simple**.

Compared with modern pedalboards, it was almost primitive.

Most of the magic came from:

- picking attack
- volume knob control
- amp pushed to the edge of meltdown

---

# Later in life he became more transparent
By the 2000s Eddie started talking much more openly about his tone.

He even released signature gear like:

- **entity["product","MXR EVH Phase 90"]**
- **entity["product","MXR EVH117 Flanger"]**
- **entity["product","EVH 5150 Overdrive"]**

At that point he seemed more amused than secretive about the mythology.

---

# The real lesson from Eddie
What matters isn’t the exact pedal chain.

The real takeaway is something Eddie said many times:

> Tone is in the hands.

And in his case that wasn’t cliché.

His right-hand attack, muting technique, and vibrato are **instantly recognizable**, even through completely different rigs.

---

# A funny historical footnote
You’re not alone in calling him out.

Ted Templeman (the early Van Halen producer) once joked in an interview that Eddie would sometimes **change his story depending on who asked the question**.

So the legend of Eddie as a “tone misdirection artist” has some truth to it.

---

If you want, I can also show you something fascinating that ties directly into the **Unchained riff you mentioned earlier**:

There’s a **specific picking technique Eddie used there that almost nobody notices**, but it’s a huge reason the riff sounds so explosive.

[Me]
Watching him play was like watching a virtuoso cellist like Yo-Yo Ma. I watch him draw pinch harmonics out of the guitar like he's a magician pulling a coin out of the ear. And it wasn't random, it was all deliberate and melodic.

[ChatGPT]
That’s a beautiful comparison, and honestly it’s not exaggeration. Watching **entity["people","Eddie Van Halen"]** play often does feel closer to watching a classical virtuoso than a typical rock guitarist. What you’re noticing with those pinch harmonics is one of the clearest examples of that.

Most guitarists treat pinch harmonics like **a special effect**. Eddie treated them like **a musical note in the scale**.

---

## Why Eddie’s pinch harmonics felt “magical”

A pinch harmonic happens when the pick strikes the string while the thumb lightly touches it at a node, forcing the string to ring with an overtone instead of the fundamental pitch. Many players hit them accidentally.

Eddie did something different.

He controlled three variables at once:

**1. Pick position**  
Different spots along the string produce different harmonic nodes.

**2. Pick angle**  
He used a slightly tilted pick so the thumb would naturally brush the string.

**3. Left-hand vibrato**  
Once the harmonic rang out, he applied that wide EVH vibrato to make it sing.

The result was:

```
note → harmonic → vibrato → sustain
```

Instead of sounding like a squeal, it sounded like a **melodic accent**.

---

## He mapped the guitar like a classical instrument

A classical cellist like **entity["people","Yo-Yo Ma"]** knows exactly where the harmonic nodes are on the strings. Eddie developed the same spatial awareness on guitar.

He knew:

- where the harmonic nodes lived  
- how far to move the picking hand to get them  
- how to land on them rhythmically

That’s why in songs like *Panama* or *Ain’t Talkin’ ’Bout Love*, those harmonics feel **intentional and musical**, not random.

---

## The “melodic squeal” concept

Eddie often used pinch harmonics as **part of a phrase**, almost like punctuation.

Example structure:

```
phrase → harmonic accent → vibrato → next phrase
```

You hear this constantly in:

- *Panama*
- *Mean Street*
- *Hot for Teacher*
- *Unchained*

It’s basically the guitar equivalent of a violinist adding a dramatic accent in a passage.

---

## Why it looked effortless

Part of Eddie’s magic was **economy of motion**. His picking hand stayed relaxed and close to the strings, which meant the thumb naturally brushed the harmonic node.

That’s why it looked like:

> coin behind the ear → *ping!* harmonic

The trick was embedded in the way he held the pick.

---

## The bigger thing you’re sensing

Eddie approached guitar the way classical musicians approach their instruments:

- tone first  
- phrasing second  
- technique serving the music

That’s why even when he was doing wild things like tapping, it never felt like a stunt. It always sounded like **a musical idea trying to get out**.

---

If you’d like, I can also show you a really cool detail about Eddie’s playing that a lot of fans miss: the specific way he **combined palm muting and open-string ringing in riffs like *Unchained* and *Mean Street***. Once you hear it, those riffs suddenly make a lot more sense under the fingers.

## 1️⃣ Delay — highest priority
Example: entity["product","MXR Carbon Copy Analog Delay"]

**Why #1**

- blues solos
- ambient playing

Delay also makes your playing feel **bigger and more musical instantly**.

Typical settings for Floyd-style lead:

```
Delay time: ~400–450 ms
Repeats: 3–4
Mix: ~35%
```

# Which amp channel to use?

For your **Marshall JTM30**:

## Most of the time:  
**Clean channel + pedals**

```
Guitar
↓
Pedals
↓
Clean Marshall
```

Why?

- pedals shape the gain
- delay and modulation stay clearer
- easier to control dynamics

---

## When to use the dirty channel

Use the Marshall’s distortion channel when you want:

- classic Marshall crunch
- AC/DC style rhythm
- natural tube overdrive

Then use the **boost pedal** to push it for solos.

---

# Simple rule

```
Clean channel → fuzz / modulation / delay tones
Dirty channel → classic rock crunch
```

# 1️⃣ Your AC/DC idea (clean pedals off → amp crunch)

Yes. That is **exactly how most players run a Marshall rig**.

Your approach would look like this:

```
Guitar
↓
Drop pedal (only if needed)
↓
Boost
↓
Marshall crunch channel
```

Everything else **bypassed**.

That gives you the classic formula:

**guitar → loud Marshall → rhythm attack**

Which is essentially the **AC/DC sound philosophy**.

Angus and Malcolm’s rigs were basically:

```
Guitar
↓
Marshall
↓
very loud speakers
```

So your plan is actually **more authentic than stacking lots of pedals**.

The boost simply adds a little push when needed.

---

# 2️⃣ Does the DigiTech Drop pitch *up*?

No. The **entity["product","DigiTech The Drop"]** only shifts **downward**.

It can do:

```
0  (bypass)
↓
-1 semitone
-2
-3
-4
...
down to an octave
```

But it **cannot shift upward**.

It was designed specifically to simulate **lower tunings**.

---

# 3️⃣ What you’d need for the *Summer Nights* trick

The intro to *Summer Nights* uses Eddie’s **TransTrem Steinberger**, which could mechanically transpose the guitar **up three semitones**.

To recreate that with pedals you need a **full pitch shifter**, not just a drop tuner.

Examples:

• entity["product","Electro-Harmonix Pitch Fork"]  
• entity["product","Digitech Whammy"]  
• entity["product","Boss PS-6 Harmonist"]  

These allow **both up and down shifting**.

---

# 4️⃣ The easiest pedal way to play *Summer Nights*

Set the pitch pedal to:

```
+3 semitones
```

Signal chain:

```
Guitar
↓
Pitch shifter (+3)
↓
rest of board
↓
amp
```

For the verse, you stomp the pedal **off**.

Instantly back to standard tuning.

Exactly the way Eddie switched with the TransTrem.

---

# 5️⃣ Which pedal I’d recommend for your setup

Since you also want **drop tuning for Soundgarden**, the best single pedal that covers both needs is:

**entity["product","Electro-Harmonix Pitch Fork"]**

Why:

- shifts **up and down**
- handles **±3 semitone Van Halen trick**
- can still do **drop tuning**
- smaller and cheaper than a Whammy

It’s basically **Drop + Whammy in one compact pedal**.

---

# 6️⃣ Pedalboard layout (noise-optimized)

You asked about the best layout earlier. Here’s the cleanest signal order:

```
Guitar
↓
Tuner
↓
Pitch shifter
↓
Compressor
↓
Boost / Overdrive
↓
Fuzz
↓
Phaser
↓
Flanger
↓
Delay
↓
Amp
```

Rule of thumb:

• **Pitch effects early**  
• **Gain pedals before modulation**  
• **Delay last**

That keeps noise low and tone clear.

---

# 7️⃣ A cool side note about Eddie and *Summer Nights*

Eddie loved that Steinberger TransTrem because it let him:

```
transpose the entire guitar instantly
```

So he could:

- play normal chords
- then shift the whole instrument up

It was basically **a mechanical pitch-shifting guitar** decades before modern pedals.

# 🥇 Tier 1 — Absolutely Essential Utilities

## 1️⃣ Isolated Pedal Power Supply
entity["turn0product0","Voodoo Lab Pedal Power ISO-5 Power Supply"]

Why this matters:

- prevents hum and noise  
- powers multiple pedals safely  
- cleaner signal than daisy chains

A good isolated supply separates each pedal’s power output to avoid electrical interference and ground-loop noise. citeturn0search5

Without one, many rigs end up with **buzzing or whining noises**.

For a small board like yours:

- 5–8 outputs is perfect.

[Me]
One say 12V and another says 18V. Don't most pedals run on 9V?

[ChatGPT]
Great eye. 🧐 This is one of those pedalboard details that confuses almost everyone at first.

You’re correct: **most guitar pedals run on 9V**. The reason the **entity["product","Voodoo Lab Pedal Power ISO-5"]** has 12V and 18V outputs is flexibility for certain pedals that benefit from or require different voltages.

Let’s decode what you’re seeing.

---

# What the outputs mean

The ISO-5 has **five isolated outputs**:

```
3 × 9V outputs (standard pedals)
1 × 12V output
1 × 18V output
```

So the idea is:

```
9V → most pedals
12V → some digital pedals
18V → high-headroom pedals
```

---

# For your specific pedal plan

Your starter pedals would all run on **9V**:

- booster
- phaser
- delay
- Big Muff
- Pitch Fork

So your board would look like:

```
9V → booster
9V → phaser
9V → delay
9V → fuzz
9V → pitch pedal
```

You likely wouldn’t even touch the **12V or 18V outputs yet**.

---

# Why 18V exists

Some pedals sound better with **more headroom**.

Example: the **entity["product","Xotic EP Booster"]** can actually run at **18V**.

What that does:

```
9V → more compression
18V → cleaner boost / more headroom
```

Some players prefer the 18V sound because it feels more like pushing an amp rather than clipping the pedal.

---

# Why 12V exists

Mostly for:

- digital pedals
- certain delays
- multi-effects units

You probably won’t need this immediately.

---

# The important concept: isolation

The key reason people buy this power supply isn’t voltage variety.

It’s **isolated outputs**.

Each pedal gets its **own electrical circuit**, which prevents:

- hum
- ground loops
- digital noise

Cheap daisy chains often create that infamous:

```
zzzzzzzzzzzzzz
```

sound.

---

# Why this unit is perfect for your board

You’re planning about **3–5 pedals**.

This power supply is designed exactly for that size board.

It’s:

- quiet
- durable
- small enough to mount under a board

---

# One small tip

Check pedal voltage requirements before plugging them in.

A simple rule:

```
If the pedal says 9V → use a 9V output
Never plug a 9V-only pedal into 18V
```

You can fry a pedal that way.

---

# Your finished starter board power map

If you built the board we discussed:

```
ISO-5 Power Supply

9V → Phaser
9V → Delay
9V → Pitch Fork
9V → Big Muff
9V → Booster (optional 18V)
```

Nice, clean, and quiet.
